### Languages

According to the 50th report of the commissioner for linguistic minorities in India, which was submitted in 2014, [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi "Hindi") is Delhi's most spoken language, with 80.94% speakers, followed by [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language "Punjabi language") (7.14%), [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu "Urdu") (6.31%) and [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language "Bengali language") (1.50%). 4.11% speak other languages.[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-langminor-233) Hindi is also the official language of Delhi while Urdu and Punjabi have been declared as additional official languages.[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-langminor-233)

## Culture

See also: [Culture of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_India "Culture of India")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Traditional_pottery_in_Dilli_Haat.jpg/500px-Traditional_pottery_in_Dilli_Haat.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Traditional_pottery_in_Dilli_Haat.jpg)

Traditional pottery on display in _[Dilli Haat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilli_Haat "Dilli Haat")_

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Pragati_Maidan%2C_inside_hall_18_%283%29.JPG/500px-Pragati_Maidan%2C_inside_hall_18_%283%29.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pragati_Maidan,_inside_hall_18_(3).JPG)

Pragati Maidan hosts the _World Book Fair_ biennially.

Delhi's culture has been influenced by its lengthy history and historic association as the capital of India. Although a strong [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabis "Punjabis") Influence can be seen in [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language "Punjabi language"), [dress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_clothing "Punjabi clothing") and [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_cuisine "Punjabi cuisine") brought by the large number of refugees who came following the [partition in 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India "Partition of India") the recent migration from other parts of India has made it a [melting pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_pot "Melting pot"). This is exemplified by many significant monuments in the city. The [Archaeological Survey of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_Survey_of_India "Archaeological Survey of India") recognises 1,200 heritage buildings[\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-234) and 175 monuments as national heritage sites.[\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-asimonuments-235)

In the Old City, the Mughals and the Turkic rulers constructed several architecturally significant buildings, such as the [Jama Masjid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jama_Masjid,_Delhi "Jama Masjid, Delhi")—India's largest mosque[\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-Jama-236) built in 1656[\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-237) and the [Red Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Fort "Red Fort"). Three [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site "World Heritage Site")—the Red Fort, [Qutub Minar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutab_Minar "Qutab Minar") and [Humayun's Tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humayun's_Tomb "Humayun's Tomb")—are located in Delhi.[\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-whsite-238) Other monuments include the [India Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_Gate "India Gate"), the [Jantar Mantar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yantra_Mandir_(Delhi) "Yantra Mandir (Delhi)")—an 18th-century astronomical observatory—and the [Purana Qila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purana_Qila "Purana Qila")—a 16th-century fortress. The [Laxminarayan Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laxminarayan_Temple "Laxminarayan Temple"), [Akshardham temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akshardham_(Delhi) "Akshardham (Delhi)"), [Gurudwara Bangla Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurudwara_Bangla_Sahib "Gurudwara Bangla Sahib"), the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith "Baháʼí Faith")'s [Lotus Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lotus_Temple "Lotus Temple") and the [ISKCON temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISKCON_Temple_Delhi "ISKCON Temple Delhi") are examples of modern architecture. [Raj Ghat and associated memorials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raj_Ghat_and_associated_memorials "Raj Ghat and associated memorials") houses memorials of [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi "Mahatma Gandhi") and other notable personalities. New Delhi houses several government buildings and official residences reminiscent of British colonial architecture, including the Rashtrapati Bhavan, [the Secretariat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_Building,_New_Delhi "Secretariat Building, New Delhi"), [Rajpath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajpath "Rajpath"), the Parliament of India and [Vijay Chowk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vijay_Chowk "Vijay Chowk"). [Safdarjung's Tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomb_of_Safdar_Jang "Tomb of Safdar Jang") is an example of the [Mughal gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_gardens "Mughal gardens") style. Some regal _havelis_ (palatial residences) are in the Old City.[\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-jacob-239) [Lotus Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lotus_Temple "Lotus Temple") is a Baháʼí House of Worship completed in 1986. Notable for its flowerlike shape, it serves as the Mother Temple of the Indian subcontinent and has become a prominent attraction in the city. The [National Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum,_New_Delhi "National Museum, New Delhi") and [National Gallery of Modern Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Modern_Art "National Gallery of Modern Art") are some of the largest museums in the country. Other museums in Delhi include the [National Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Natural_History,_New_Delhi "National Museum of Natural History, New Delhi"), [National Rail Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rail_Museum,_New_Delhi "National Rail Museum, New Delhi") and [National Philatelic Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Philatelic_Museum,_New_Delhi "National Philatelic Museum, New Delhi").\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

[Chandni Chowk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandni_Chowk "Chandni Chowk"), a 17th-century market, is one of the most popular shopping areas in Delhi for jewellery and _Zari_ [saris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sari "Sari").[\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-Chandni-240) Delhi's arts and crafts include, _[Zardozi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zardozi "Zardozi")_[\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-241)—an embroidery done with gold thread[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-242)—and _[Meenakari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meenakari "Meenakari")_[\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-243)—the art of enamelling.

- [![The Birla temple in Delhi with its towers.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/70/Birla_Mandir_Delhi.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Birla_Mandir_Delhi.jpg "The Hindu Laxminarayan Mandir was inaugurated by Mahatma Gandhi in 1933.")

  The Hindu [Laxminarayan Mandir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laxminarayan_Temple "Laxminarayan Temple") was inaugurated by [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi "Mahatma Gandhi") in 1933.

- [![The Jama Masjid was built by the Mughal Emperor Shah Jahan between 1650 and 1656.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Jama_Masjid_-_In_the_Noon.jpg/960px-Jama_Masjid_-_In_the_Noon.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jama_Masjid_-_In_the_Noon.jpg "The Jama Masjid was built by the Mughal Emperor Shah Jahan between 1650 and 1656.")

  The [Jama Masjid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jama_Masjid,_Delhi "Jama Masjid, Delhi") was built by the [Mughal Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Emperor "Mughal Emperor") [Shah Jahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Jahan "Shah Jahan") between 1650 and 1656.

- [![The prayer hall of Sikh Gurudwara Sis Ganj Sahib in Chandni Chowk, Old Delhi which dates to 1783](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Interior-view-Gurudwara-Sis-Ganj-Sahib-Night-View.jpg/960px-Interior-view-Gurudwara-Sis-Ganj-Sahib-Night-View.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Interior-view-Gurudwara-Sis-Ganj-Sahib-Night-View.jpg "The prayer hall of Sikh Gurudwara Sis Ganj Sahib in Chandni Chowk, Old Delhi which dates to 1783")

  The prayer hall of Sikh [Gurudwara Sis Ganj Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurudwara_Sis_Ganj_Sahib "Gurudwara Sis Ganj Sahib") in [Chandni Chowk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandni_Chowk "Chandni Chowk"), [Old Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Delhi "Old Delhi") which dates to 1783


### Festivals

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Chhath_Puja_Sandya_Aragh-005.jpg/500px-Chhath_Puja_Sandya_Aragh-005.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Chhath_Puja_Sandya_Aragh-005.jpg)

More than a quarter of the immigrants in Delhi are from [Bihar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bihar "Bihar") and neighbouring states. [Chhath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chhath "Chhath"), a festival of Bihar is now popular in Delhi.[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-kapur-busstand-chhath-244)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Basant_Celebrations_at_Dargah.jpg/500px-Basant_Celebrations_at_Dargah.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Basant_Celebrations_at_Dargah.jpg)

On [Basant Panchmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basant_Panchmi "Basant Panchmi") eve, [qawwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qawwali "Qawwali") singers wearing yellow headbands gather at the [dargah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dargah "Dargah") of [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism "Sufism") saint [Nizamuddin Auliya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizamuddin_Auliya "Nizamuddin Auliya") to sing verses from [Amir Khusrau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amir_Khusrau "Amir Khusrau").[\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-tiwari-wire-auliya-245)

Delhi's association and geographic proximity to the capital, New Delhi, has amplified the importance of national events and holidays like [Republic Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_Day_(India) "Republic Day (India)"), [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_(India) "Independence Day (India)") (15 August) and _[Gandhi Jayanti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhi_Jayanti "Gandhi Jayanti")_. On Independence Day, the Prime Minister addresses the nation from the Red Fort. The [Republic Day Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Republic_Day_parade "Delhi Republic Day parade") is a large cultural and military parade showcasing India's cultural diversity and military strength.[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-repmil-246)[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-repcul-247) Over the centuries, Delhi has become known for its composite culture, and a festival that symbolises this is the _[Phool Walon Ki Sair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phool_Walon_Ki_Sair "Phool Walon Ki Sair")_, which takes place in September. Flowers and _pankhe_—fans embroidered with flowers—are offered to the shrine of the 13th-century Sufi saint [Khwaja Bakhtiyar Kaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwaja_Bakhtiyar_Kaki "Khwaja Bakhtiyar Kaki") and the [Yogmaya Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogmaya_Temple "Yogmaya Temple"), both situated in [Mehrauli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehrauli "Mehrauli").[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-248)

Religious festivals include _[Diwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diwali "Diwali")_ (the festival of lights), _[Mahavir Jayanti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahavir_Jayanti "Mahavir Jayanti")_, [Guru Nanak's Birthday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak's_Birthday "Guru Nanak's Birthday"), _[Raksha Bandhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raksha_Bandhan "Raksha Bandhan")_, _[Durga Puja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durga_Puja "Durga Puja")_, _[Holi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holi "Holi")_, _[Lohri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lohri "Lohri")_, _[Chauth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chauth "Chauth")_, _[Krishna Janmastami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krishna_Janmastami "Krishna Janmastami")_, _[Maha Shivratri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maha_Shivratri "Maha Shivratri")_, [Eid ul-Fitr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eid_ul-Fitr "Eid ul-Fitr"), _[Moharram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Ashura "Day of Ashura")_ and _[Buddha Jayanti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha_Jayanti "Buddha Jayanti")_.[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-repcul-247) The [Qutub Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutub_Festival "Qutub Festival") is a cultural event during which performances of musicians and dancers from all over India are showcased at night, with the Qutub Minar as a backdrop.[\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-qutubfest-249) Other events such as Kite Flying Festival, [International Mango Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Mango_Festival,_Delhi "International Mango Festival, Delhi") and _[Vasant Panchami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasant_Panchami "Vasant Panchami")_ (the Spring Festival) are held every year in Delhi. The [Auto Expo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto_Expo "Auto Expo"), Asia's largest auto show,[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-autogenerated2-250) is held in Delhi biennially. The [New Delhi World Book Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Delhi_World_Book_Fair "New Delhi World Book Fair"), held biennially at the [Pragati Maidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pragati_Maidan "Pragati Maidan"), is the second-largest exhibition of books in the world.[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-indiatimes1-251) Delhi is often regarded as the "Book Capital" of India because of high readership.[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-252) [India International Trade Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_International_Trade_Fair "India International Trade Fair") (IITF), organised by [ITPO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_Trade_Promotion_Organisation "India Trade Promotion Organisation") is the biggest cultural and shopping fair of Delhi which takes place in November each year and is visited by more than 1.5million people.[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-253)

### Cuisine

Main article: [Indian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_cuisine "Indian cuisine")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Karim%27s_Hotel%2C_Delhi%284%29.jpg/500px-Karim%27s_Hotel%2C_Delhi%284%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Karim's_Hotel,_Delhi(4).jpg) Kitchen, [Karim's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim's "Karim's"), Old Delhi, a historic [restaurant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restaurant "Restaurant"), established 1913[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-254)

As India's national capital and centuries old [Mughal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_empire "Mughal empire") capital, Delhi influenced the food habits of its residents and is where [Mughlai cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughlai_cuisine "Mughlai cuisine") originated. Along with Indian cuisine, a variety of international cuisines are popular among the residents.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-Narayan-255) This variety of cuisines created a unique style of cooking which became popular throughout the world, with dishes such as _[Kebab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebab "Kebab")_, _[biryani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biryani "Biryani")_, _[tandoori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tandoor "Tandoor")_. The city's classic dishes include [butter chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butter_chicken "Butter chicken"), _[dal makhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dal_makhani "Dal makhani")_, _[shahi paneer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahi_paneer "Shahi paneer")_, _[aloo chaat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aloo_chaat "Aloo chaat")_, _[chaat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaat "Chaat")_, _[dahi bhalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahi_bhalla "Dahi bhalla")_, _[kachori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kachori "Kachori")_, _[gol gappe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panipuri "Panipuri")_, _[samosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samosa "Samosa")_, _[chole bhature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chole_bhature "Chole bhature")_, _chole kulche_, _[gulab jamun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulab_jamun "Gulab jamun")_, _[jalebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalebi "Jalebi")_ and _[lassi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lassi "Lassi")_.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-Narayan-255)[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-commonwealth-256):40–50,189–196

The fast living habits of Delhi's people has motivated the growth of [street food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_food "Street food") outlets.[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-commonwealth-256):41 A trend of dining at local _[dhabas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaba "Dhaba")_ is popular among the residents. High-profile restaurants have gained popularity in recent years, among the popular restaurants are the Karim Hotel, the Punjab Grill and Bukhara.[\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-Telegraph-257) The _[Gali Paranthe Wali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gali_Paranthe_Wali "Gali Paranthe Wali")_ (the street of fried bread) is a street in Chandni Chowk particularly for food eateries since the 1870s. Almost the entire street is occupied by fast food stalls or [street vendors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawker_(trade) "Hawker (trade)"). Other Indian cuisines are also available in this area even though the street specialises in north Indian food.[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-commonwealth-256):40–50[\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-258)

## Education

Main articles: [Education in Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Delhi "Education in Delhi") and [List of schools in Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_schools_in_Delhi "List of schools in Delhi")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Lady_Hardinge_Medical_College_and_Hospital%2C_Delhi%3B_pharmacy._Wellcome_V0029217.jpg/250px-Lady_Hardinge_Medical_College_and_Hospital%2C_Delhi%3B_pharmacy._Wellcome_V0029217.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lady_Hardinge_Medical_College_and_Hospital,_Delhi;_pharmacy._Wellcome_V0029217.jpg)

[Lady Hardinge Medical College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_Hardinge_Medical_College "Lady Hardinge Medical College") pharmacy, 1921

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/IP_COLLEGE_FOR_WOMEN.jpg/330px-IP_COLLEGE_FOR_WOMEN.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:IP_COLLEGE_FOR_WOMEN.jpg)

[Indraprastha College for Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indraprastha_College_for_Women "Indraprastha College for Women"), established in 1924

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Mohibul_Hasan_House%2C_Faculty_of_Humanities_and_Languages%2C_JMI.jpg/330px-Mohibul_Hasan_House%2C_Faculty_of_Humanities_and_Languages%2C_JMI.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mohibul_Hasan_House,_Faculty_of_Humanities_and_Languages,_JMI.jpg)

[Jamia Millia Islamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamia_Millia_Islamia "Jamia Millia Islamia") est. 1920 by [M. A. Ansari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M._A._Ansari "M. A. Ansari") and [Zakir Husain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakir_Husain "Zakir Husain")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Delhiuni.jpg/330px-Delhiuni.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Delhiuni.jpg)

[University of Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Delhi "University of Delhi") was founded in 1922. Sir [Maurice Gwyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Gwyer "Maurice Gwyer") served as its first [vice-chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice-chancellor "Vice-chancellor").

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Anglo_Arabic_School%27s_Hostel_earlier_known_as_Ghaziuddin_Khan_Madrasa.jpg/330px-Anglo_Arabic_School%27s_Hostel_earlier_known_as_Ghaziuddin_Khan_Madrasa.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Anglo_Arabic_School's_Hostel_earlier_known_as_Ghaziuddin_Khan_Madrasa.jpg)

[Dormitory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dormitory "Dormitory") of [Anglo Arabic Senior Secondary School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo_Arabic_Senior_Secondary_School "Anglo Arabic Senior Secondary School"), founded 1696, reorganised 1828

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Delhi_Government_School_Student_defining_Happiness_in_Happiness_Class.jpg/330px-Delhi_Government_School_Student_defining_Happiness_in_Happiness_Class.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Delhi_Government_School_Student_defining_Happiness_in_Happiness_Class.jpg)

A Delhi government school student writing down the names of fruits and vegetables

Private schools in Delhi—which use either English or Hindi as the language of instruction—are affiliated to one of three administering bodies, the [Council for the Indian School Certificate Examinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_for_the_Indian_School_Certificate_Examinations "Council for the Indian School Certificate Examinations") (CISCE), the [Central Board for Secondary Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Board_for_Secondary_Education "Central Board for Secondary Education") (CBSE)[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-259) or the [National Institute of Open Schooling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institute_of_Open_Schooling "National Institute of Open Schooling") (NIOS). In 2004–05, approximately 1,529,000 students were enrolled in primary schools, 822,000 in middle schools and 669,000 in secondary schools across Delhi.[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-ecosurv15-260) Female students represented 49% of the total enrolment. The same year, the Delhi government spent between 1.58% and 1.95% of its gross state domestic product on education.[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-ecosurv15-260)

[Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School#India "School") and higher educational institutions in Delhi are administered either by the [Directorate of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Directorate_of_Education "Delhi Directorate of Education"), the NCT government or private organisations. In 2006, Delhi had 165 colleges, five medical colleges and eight engineering colleges,[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-ecosurv15-260) seven major universities and nine [deemed universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deemed_University "Deemed University").[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-ecosurv15-260)

The premier management colleges of Delhi such as [Faculty of Management Studies (Delhi)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faculty_of_Management_Studies_(Delhi) "Faculty of Management Studies (Delhi)") and [Indian Institute of Foreign Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Institute_of_Foreign_Trade "Indian Institute of Foreign Trade") rank the best in India. [All India Institute of Medical Sciences Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Institute_of_Medical_Sciences_Delhi "All India Institute of Medical Sciences Delhi") is a premier medical school for treatment and research. [National Law University, Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Law_University,_Delhi "National Law University, Delhi") is a prominent law school and is affiliated with the [Bar Council of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_Council_of_India "Bar Council of India"). The [Indian Institute of Technology, Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Institute_of_Technology,_Delhi "Indian Institute of Technology, Delhi") situated in [Hauz Khas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauz_Khas "Hauz Khas") is a premier engineering college of India and ranks as one of the top institutes in South Asia.[\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-261)[\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-262)

[Delhi Technological University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Technological_University "Delhi Technological University"), [Indira Gandhi Delhi Technical University for Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi_Delhi_Technical_University_for_Women "Indira Gandhi Delhi Technical University for Women"), [Indraprastha Institute of Information Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indraprastha_Institute_of_Information_Technology "Indraprastha Institute of Information Technology"), [Netaji Subhas University of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhas_University_of_Technology "Netaji Subhas University of Technology"), [Guru Gobind Singh Indraprastha University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh_Indraprastha_University "Guru Gobind Singh Indraprastha University") and [National Law University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Law_University "National Law University") are the only state universities.[\[258\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-263)[\[259\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-264) [University of Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Delhi "University of Delhi"), [Jawaharlal Nehru University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru_University,_Delhi "Jawaharlal Nehru University, Delhi") and [Jamia Millia Islamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamia_Millia_Islamia "Jamia Millia Islamia") are the [central universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_University_(India) "Central University (India)"), and [Indira Gandhi National Open University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi_National_Open_University "Indira Gandhi National Open University") is for [distance education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distance_education "Distance education").[\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-Act-265)As of 2008[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Delhi&action=edit), about 16% of all Delhi residents possessed at least a college graduate degree.[\[261\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-266)

According to the Directorate of Education and [GNCTD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Delhi "Government of Delhi") the following languages are taught in schools in Delhi under the [three-language formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-language_formula "Three-language formula"):[\[262\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-267)

- First language: [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi "Hindi"), [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu "Urdu"), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language "English language")
- Second language: [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language "English language")
- Third language: [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu "Urdu"), [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language "Punjabi language"), [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language "Bengali language"), [Sindhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_language "Sindhi language"), [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_Nadu "Tamil Nadu"), [Telugu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telugu_language "Telugu language"), [Malayalam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayalam "Malayalam"), [Kannada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kannada "Kannada"), [Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_language "Gujarati language"), [Marathi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathi_language "Marathi language"), [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit"), [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language "Persian language"), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic "Arabic")

## Media

See also: [Media of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_India "Media of India")

[![Pitampura TV Tower with background of blue sky](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Pitampura_TV_Tower%2C_Delhi%2C_India.jpg/500px-Pitampura_TV_Tower%2C_Delhi%2C_India.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pitampura_TV_Tower,_Delhi,_India.jpg) [Pitampura TV Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitampura_TV_Tower "Pitampura TV Tower") broadcasts to Delhi.

As the capital of India, Delhi is the focus of political reportage, including regular television broadcasts of Parliament sessions. Many national media agencies, including the state-owned [Press Trust of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Trust_of_India "Press Trust of India"), Media Trust of India and [Doordarshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doordarshan "Doordarshan"), are based in the city. Television programming includes two free terrestrial television channels offered by Doordarshan, and several Hindi, English, and regional-language [cable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_television "Cable television") channels offered by [multi system operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi_system_operator "Multi system operator"). [Satellite television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_broadcast_satellite "Direct broadcast satellite") has yet to gain a large number of subscribers in the city.[\[263\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-dthrediff-268)

Print journalism remains a popular news medium in Delhi. The city's Hindi newspapers include _[Navbharat Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navbharat_Times "Navbharat Times")_, _[Hindustan Dainik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustan_Dainik "Hindustan Dainik")_, _[Punjab Kesari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab_Kesari "Punjab Kesari")_, _Pavitra Bharat_, _[Dainik Jagran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dainik_Jagran "Dainik Jagran")_, _[Dainik Bhaskar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dainik_Bhaskar "Dainik Bhaskar")_, _[Amar Ujala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amar_Ujala "Amar Ujala")_ and _Dainik Desbandhu_. Among the English language newspapers, the _[Hindustan Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustan_Times "Hindustan Times")_, with a daily circulation of over a million copies, is the single largest daily.[\[264\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-269) Other major English newspapers include _[The Times of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times_of_India "The Times of India")_, _[The Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hindu "The Hindu")_, _[The Indian Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Indian_Express "The Indian Express")_, _[Business Standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Standard "Business Standard")_, _[The Pioneer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pioneer_(daily) "The Pioneer (daily)")_, _[The Statesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Statesman_(India) "The Statesman (India)")_, and _[The Asian Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Asian_Age "The Asian Age")_. Regional language newspapers include the [Malayalam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayalam_language "Malayalam language") daily _[Malayala Manorama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayala_Manorama "Malayala Manorama")_ and the [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language "Tamil language") dailies _[Dinamalar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinamalar "Dinamalar")_ and _[Dinakaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinakaran "Dinakaran")_. [Qaumi Duniya Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qaumi_Duniya_Daily?action=edit&redlink=1 "Qaumi Duniya Daily") was a local Urdu newspaper, which has since moved online.

Radio is a less popular mass medium in Delhi, although [FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frequency_modulation "Frequency modulation") radio has gained popularity[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-radiomass-270) since the inauguration of several new stations in 2006.[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-asiawaves-271) A number of state-owned and private radio stations broadcast from Delhi.[\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-272)[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-asiawaves-271)

## Sports

Main articles: [Sport in Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Delhi "Sport in Delhi"), [1951 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Asian_Games "1951 Asian Games"), [1982 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Asian_Games "1982 Asian Games"), and [2010 Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Commonwealth_Games "2010 Commonwealth Games")

See also: [Culture of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_India "Culture of India")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/2f/Indian_athletes_at_the_First_Asiad.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Indian_athletes_at_the_First_Asiad.png)

Indian athletes marching into the [National Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_Dhyan_Chand_National_Stadium "Major Dhyan Chand National Stadium") during the opening ceremony of the [1951 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Asian_Games "1951 Asian Games")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Jawaharlal_Nehru_Stadium_CWG_opening_ceremony.jpg/500px-Jawaharlal_Nehru_Stadium_CWG_opening_ceremony.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jawaharlal_Nehru_Stadium_CWG_opening_ceremony.jpg)

[Jawaharlal Nehru Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru_Stadium,_Delhi "Jawaharlal Nehru Stadium, Delhi") on the night of the [2010 Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Commonwealth_Games "2010 Commonwealth Games") opening ceremony

Delhi hosted the first Asian Games in 1951 from 4 to 11 March. A total of 489 athletes representing 11 Asian [National Olympic Committees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Olympic_Committees "National Olympic Committees") participated in 57 events from eight [sports and discipline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Games_sports "Asian Games sports"). The Games was the successor of the [Far Eastern Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_Eastern_Games "Far Eastern Games") and the revival of the [Western Asiatic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asiatic_Games "Western Asiatic Games"). On 13 February 1949, the [Asian Games Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Games_Federation "Asian Games Federation") was formally established in Delhi, with Delhi unanimously announced as the first host city of the [Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Games "Asian Games"). [National Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhyan_Chand_National_Stadium "Dhyan Chand National Stadium") was the venue for all events.[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-273) Over 40,000 spectators watched the opening ceremony of the Games in National Stadium.[\[269\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-274)

Delhi hosted the ninth Asian Games for the second time in 1982 from 19 November to 4 December. This was the second time the city has hosted the Asian Games and was also the first Asian Games to be held under the aegis of the [Olympic Council of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Council_of_Asia "Olympic Council of Asia"). A total of 3,411 athletes from 33 [National Olympic Committees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Olympic_Committee "National Olympic Committee") participated in these games, competing in 196 events in 21 sports and 23 disciplines. The [Jawaharlal Nehru Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru_Stadium_(Delhi) "Jawaharlal Nehru Stadium (Delhi)"), which has a capacity of 60,000 people, was built purposely for the event and hosted its opening ceremony.[\[270\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-275)

Delhi hosted the [Nineteenth Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Commonwealth_Games "2010 Commonwealth Games") in 2010, which ran from 3 to 14 October and was the largest sporting event held in India.[\[271\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-276)[\[272\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-277) The opening ceremony of the 2010 Commonwealth Games was held at the [Jawaharlal Nehru Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru_Stadium,_Delhi "Jawaharlal Nehru Stadium, Delhi"), the main stadium of the event, in New Delhi at 7:00pm [Indian Standard Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Standard_Time "Indian Standard Time") on 3 October 2010.[\[273\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-278) The ceremony featured over 8,000 performers and lasted for two and a half hours.[\[274\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-279) It is estimated that ₹3.5 billion (US$37million) were spent to produce the ceremony.[\[275\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-280) Events took place at 12 competition venues. 20 training venues were used in the Games, including seven venues within [Delhi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_University "Delhi University").[\[276\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-Non-Competition_Venues-281) The rugby stadium in Delhi University North Campus hosted rugby games for Commonwealth Games.[\[276\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-Non-Competition_Venues-281)

[Cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket "Cricket") and [football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_football "Association football") are the most popular sports in Delhi.[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-popular-282) There are several cricket grounds, or _[maidans](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/maidan "wiktionary:maidan")_, located across the city. The [Arun Jaitley Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arun_Jaitley_Stadium "Arun Jaitley Stadium") (known commonly as the _Kotla_) is one of the oldest cricket grounds in India and is a venue for international cricket matches. It is the home ground of [Delhi cricket team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_cricket_team "Delhi cricket team") and the [Indian Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Premier_League "Indian Premier League") franchise [Delhi Capitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Capitals "Delhi Capitals").[\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-283) The Delhi cricket team represents the city in the Indian domestic tournaments.[\[279\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-ranji-284) It has produced several world-class international cricketers such as [Virender Sehwag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virender_Sehwag "Virender Sehwag"), [Virat Kohli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virat_Kohli "Virat Kohli"),[\[280\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-285) [Gautam Gambhir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautam_Gambhir "Gautam Gambhir"), [Madan Lal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madan_Lal "Madan Lal"), [Chetan Chauhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chetan_Chauhan "Chetan Chauhan"), [Shikhar Dhawan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shikhar_Dhawan "Shikhar Dhawan"), [Ishant Sharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishant_Sharma "Ishant Sharma"), [Manoj Prabhakar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manoj_Prabhakar "Manoj Prabhakar") and [Bishan Singh Bedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishan_Singh_Bedi "Bishan Singh Bedi") to name a few. The [Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railways_cricket_team "Railways cricket team") and [Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Services_cricket_team "Services cricket team") cricket teams of domestic circuit also play their home matches in Delhi, at the [Karnail Singh Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karnail_Singh_Stadium "Karnail Singh Stadium") and the [Palam A Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palam_A_Stadium "Palam A Stadium"), respectively.[\[281\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-286)

[Ambedkar Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambedkar_Stadium "Ambedkar Stadium"), a football stadium in Delhi which holds 21,000 people, was the venue for the Indian football team's World Cup qualifier against UAE on 28 July 2012.[\[282\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-287) Delhi hosted the Nehru Cup in [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Nehru_Cup "2007 Nehru Cup")[\[283\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-288) and [2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Nehru_Cup "2009 Nehru Cup"), in both of which India defeated [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria "Syria") 1–0.[\[284\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-289) Delhi's professional association football club [Delhi FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_FC "Delhi FC") competes in [I-League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-League "I-League").[\[285\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-290) In the [Elite Football League of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elite_Football_League_of_India "Elite Football League of India"), Delhi's first professional [American football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_football "American football") franchise, the [Delhi Defenders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Defenders "Delhi Defenders") played its first season in [Pune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pune "Pune").[\[286\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-291) [Buddh International Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddh_International_Circuit "Buddh International Circuit") in Greater Noida, a suburb of Delhi, formerly hosted the [Formula 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_1 "Formula 1") [Indian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Grand_Prix "Indian Grand Prix").[\[287\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-292)

2. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-25)[/ˈdɛli/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English"); Hindi pronunciation:[\[ˈdɪlːiː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Hindi_and_Urdu "Help:IPA/Hindi and Urdu") _dillī_, occasionally [\[ˈdeːɦliː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Hindi_and_Urdu "Help:IPA/Hindi and Urdu") _dehlī_; Punjabi pronunciation:[\[ˈdɪlːiː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Punjabi "Help:IPA/Punjabi") _dillī_; Urdu pronunciation:[\[ˈdeɦliː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Hindi_and_Urdu "Help:IPA/Hindi and Urdu") _dêhlī_, informally [\[ˈdɪlːiː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Hindi_and_Urdu "Help:IPA/Hindi and Urdu") _dillī_[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-22)[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-23)[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-habib,_Royal,_karamchandani,_National-24)
3. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-188)The elevated Delhi metro is seen above in [Azadpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azadpur "Azadpur").[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-cities.expressindia.com-134)[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-187)
4. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-fn1_216-0)Transfer stations are counted more than once. There are 24 transfer stations. If transfer stations are counted only once, the result will be 230 stations. [Ashok Park Main](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashok_Park_Main_metro_station "Ashok Park Main metro station") station, where the two diverging branches of [Green Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Line_(Delhi_Metro) "Green Line (Delhi Metro)") share tracks/platforms, is anyway counted as a single station. Stations of [Noida Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqua_Line_(Noida_Metro) "Aqua Line (Noida Metro)") and [Gurgaon Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Metro_Gurgaon "Rapid Metro Gurgaon") are not counted. If stations of [Noida Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqua_Line_(Noida_Metro) "Aqua Line (Noida Metro)") and [Gurgaon Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Metro_Gurgaon "Rapid Metro Gurgaon") are counted, the result will be 286 stations[\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-route_map-214)[\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-present_network-213)[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-introduction-215)
5. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-fn2_217-0)The total length of Delhi Metro is 348.12 kilometres (216.31mi). The operations & maintenance of [Gurgaon Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Metro_Gurgaon "Rapid Metro Gurgaon") and [Noida Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noida_Metro "Noida Metro") is currently undertaken by [DMRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Metro_Rail_Corporation "Delhi Metro Rail Corporation"), so the total length operated by DMRC is 390.14 kilometres (242.42mi).[\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-route_map-214)[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_note-introduction-215)

Delhi has an area of 1,483 sq. km. Its population (2011 census) is 16,787,941.

026. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-sunil-kumar2_28-0)Kumar, Sunil (2013), "Delhi", in Bowering, Gerhard; Crone, Patricia; Mirza, Mahan (eds.), _The Princeton Encyclopedia of Islamic Political Thought_, Princeton University Press, p.127, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [9780691134840](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780691134840 "Special:BookSources/9780691134840"), (page 126) Although the ancient history of Delhi is always linked with Indraprastha, the capital of the Pandawas in the Mahabharata epic, (ca. 1500–1000 BCE), no archaeological trace of the city has been discovered. Instead a variety of smaller settlements attest to the occupation of the area from the fifth century BCE without leading to full scale urbanization until at the earliest, the 11th and 12th centuries CE, when the Tomara and Chawhan chieftains established their relatively humble headquarters in the southern reaches of the Delhi plain.
027. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-29)

     - Talbot, Ian; Singh, Gurharpal (2009), [_The Partition of India_](https://books.google.com/books?id=utKmPQAACAAJ&pg=PA118), Cambridge University Press, pp.118–119, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-0-521-85661-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-521-85661-4 "Special:BookSources/978-0-521-85661-4"), [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20211202201454/https://books.google.com/books?id=utKmPQAACAAJ&pg=PA118) from the original on 2 December 2021, retrieved 3 December 2021, It is now almost a cliché that the Partition transformed Delhi from a Mughal to a Punjabi city. The bitter experiences of the refugees at the hands of Islamists in Pakistan encouraged them to support right-wing Hindu parties. ... Trouble began in September (1947) after the arrival of refugees from Pakistan who were determined on revenge and driving Muslims out of properties which they could then occupy. Gandhi in his prayer meetings in Birla House denounced the 'crooked and ungentlemanly' squeezing out of Muslims who left for Pakistan. Despite these exhortations, two-thirds of the city's Muslims were to abandon India's capital eventually.
     - Sen, Uditi (2017). "Building Bijaygarh: A Microhistory of Refugee Squatting in Calcutta". In Bandyopadhyay, Sekhar; Sarkar, Tanika (eds.). _Calcutta: The Stormy Decades_. Routledge. p.407\. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [9781351581721](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/9781351581721 "Special:BookSources/9781351581721"). Historians of the Partition of India broadly acknowledge that the demographic upheaval that followed in its wake permanently altered the faces of the capital cities of South Asia. Karachi and Dhaka were transformed overnight into national capitals; Delhi changed from a Mughal city to a Punjabi one, while Calcutta, which had always been a city of migrants, rapidly earned the dubious distinction of becoming a city of refugees.
     - Virdee, Pippa (2023), "Partitioning India: Dreams, memories, and legacies", in Jacobsen, Knut A. (ed.), _Routledge Handbook of Contemporary India_, Routledge, [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"): [10.4324/9781003278436-3](https://doi.org/10.4324%2F9781003278436-3), [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [9781000984231](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/9781000984231 "Special:BookSources/9781000984231"), The mass migration of people following Partition also fundamentally altered the physical landscape. Delhi, the colonial capital, was transformed from an Urdu-speaking Muslim city in character to a city that housed the incoming Punjabi refugees that now lend much of their character to the city. … Delhi retains its Mughal links through the extensive architecture that dominates the city, but the people are now heavily influenced by Punjabi migrants. Mughal place names and influences though are gradually being erased in favour of more 'ancient' and 'authentic' alternatives.
     - Guha, Ramchandra (2011), _India after Gandhi: The History of the World's Largest Democracy_, Pan Macmillan, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [9780330540209](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780330540209 "Special:BookSources/9780330540209"), Meanwhile, thousands of refugees had made their homes in Delhi itself. Till 1911 that city had been Muslim in character and culture. In that year, the British shifted their capital there from Calcutta. After 1947 New Delhi became the seat of the government of free India. Urdu-speaking Muslims went away to Pakistan, many unwillingly, while Punjabi-speaking Hindus and Sikhas arrived in their place. They set up house, and shop, wherever they could. … Indeed, a city that was once a Mughal city, then a British city, had by the 1950s emphatically become a Punjabi city.

028. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-30) A large number of refugees migrated out of Sindh, Pakistan in 1947. They were found living in Gujarat, Rajasthan, Hyderabad, and Maharashtra:


     - Gautam, Khushboo; Devnani, Chanchal; Dasgupta, Shruti; Reddy, Madhusudan (2025). ["Genetic architecture of the Sindhi Indian population: a 19X-STR forensic analysis"](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12505593). _BMC Genomics_. **26** (889) 889. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"):[10.1186/s12864-025-12032-8](https://doi.org/10.1186%2Fs12864-025-12032-8). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_(identifier) "PMC (identifier)") [12505593](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12505593). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_(identifier) "PMID (identifier)") [41057809](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41057809). A large number of Sindhi migrated from Pakistan after the partition of India in 1947 and were found inhabiting Gujarat, Rajasthan, Hyderabad, and Maharashtra.
     - Kumar, Priya; Kumar, Rita (2016). "Sindh, 1947 and Beyond". _South Asia: Journal of South Asian Studies_. **39** (4). Routledge: 773–789\. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"): [10.1080/00856401.2016.1244752](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F00856401.2016.1244752). Meanwhile, the modalities of travel from Sindh also ensured relative safety. A large number of Sindhi Hindus travelled by sea, arriving directly at the ports of Bombay (now Mumbai), Porbandar, Veraval and Okha in Gujarat, enabling them to avoid the dangers of border crossing. Those who travelled by train from the interior of Sindh arrived in Rajasthan, also evading the violence in parts of North India. A smaller number crossed the Thar Desert border area by camel. This occludes from the Sindhi experience the metaphor of the death trains, a central trope of much Partition literature and film.
     - Kumar, Lomous; Nongmaithem, Suraj; Kumar, Sachin; Thangaraj, Kumarasamy (2025). ["Investigating the demographic history of Sindhi population inhabited in West Coast India"](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12487577). _Human Genomics_. **19** (111) 111. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"):[10.1186/s40246-025-00822-w](https://doi.org/10.1186%2Fs40246-025-00822-w). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_(identifier) "PMC (identifier)") [12487577](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12487577). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_(identifier) "PMID (identifier)") [41029758](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41029758). (Introduction, page 2) During Partition, a large number (about a million) of non-Muslim Sindhi migrated to Gujarat, Rajasthan, and Punjab.
     - Ansari, Sarah; Gould, William (2019). _Boundaries of Belonging: Localities, Citizenship and Rights in India and Pakistan_. Cambridge University Press. p.71\. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"): [10.1017/9781108164511.004](https://doi.org/10.1017%2F9781108164511.004). Though a definitive total is impossible to provide thanks to the massive demographic confusion that Partition produced, an estimated fourteen to sixteen million people sought to cross the hastily delineated borders in what remains arguably the largest migration in history. ... people crossed the Rajasthan border into and out of Sindh or travelled by ship between the port cities of Bombay and Karachi.

029. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-31)

     - Talbot, Ian (2020), _The History of British Diplomacy in Pakistan_, Routledge, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [9781000326703](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/9781000326703 "Special:BookSources/9781000326703"), Delhi had also experienced a refugee influx that transformed it from a Mughal city to a Punjabi city in the aftermath of Partition. Despite the departure of around 300,000 Muslims, the city's population grew dramatically with attendant pressures on housing. By the beginning of August 1947, there were already 80,000 refugees in the city, and after the Transfer of Power, this number was swollen daily by around 4,000–5,000 migrants from Pakistan Punjab. The 1951 Census revealed that every third person in the city was a partition migrant.
     - Kumar, Sunil (2013), "Delhi", in Bowering, Gerhard; Crone, Patricia; Mirza, Mahan (eds.), _The Princeton Encyclopedia of Islamic Political Thought_, Princeton University Press, p.127, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [9780691134840](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780691134840 "Special:BookSources/9780691134840"), ... it was not until 1648 and the construction of the new city in the northern part of the riverine plain by Shibab al-Did Shah Jahan that the imperial capital returned to Delhi. The new capital was named Shahajahanabad … was the largest, most complex, and expansive city to be constructed in the Delhi region. … The demise of Shahjahanabad as a center of culture, social life, and political authority was confirmed when the British started constructing New Delhi as their capital. The new colonial capital was modeled on architectural paradigms first tested in South Africa and Australia and, other than in its decorative aspects, retained little of the urban traditions of the Delhi Sultanate or Shajahanabad. Independent India inherited this city as its capital in 1947, a transition that was disrupted by partition and communal clashes with large numbers of the city's Muslim population fled and were replaced by displaced Punjabi refugees from West Pakistan. The demographic change in the population brought new residents to the city who were far removed from its history and culture. _Hazrat-i-Dehli_ meant little to the new residents of the capital of independent India, a past of the city that resides uneasily with its present.
     - Geva, Rotem (2024). "Press charges: renegotiating free speech and citizenship in post-partition Delhi". _Contemporary South Asia_. **32** (2). Routledge: 137–150\. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"): [10.1080/09584935.2024.2341175](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F09584935.2024.2341175). This article analyzes the conflict between press censorship and press freedom in Delhi during the period immediately after partition, …. Decolonization had a momentous impact on India's capital city, Delhi, resulting in structural shifts and dislocations. As the seat of political power and an emblem of the nation, Delhi was central to the projects of nation building and state formation, with their attendant ideological and power struggles. At the same time, Delhi became a 'partition city,' overwhelmed with roughly half a million Hindu and Sikh refugees from West Pakistan, desperate for food, shelter, and livelihood. Amid this crisis, major violence erupted, targeting Delhi's Muslims and forcing over two-thirds of them to flee to Pakistan.

076. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-guardian-3-16-20-1_78-0)Ellis-Peterson, Hannah; Azizur Rahman, Shaikh (16 March 2020), ["Delhi's Muslims despair of justice after police implicated in riots"](https://www.theguardian.com/world/2020/mar/16/delhis-muslims-despair-justice-police-implicated-hindu-riots), _The Guardian_, Delhi, [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200317023019/https://www.theguardian.com/world/2020/mar/16/delhis-muslims-despair-justice-police-implicated-hindu-riots) from the original on 17 March 2020, retrieved 17 March 2020, As the mob attacks came once, then twice and then a third time in this north-east Delhi neighbourhood, desperate stallholders repeatedly ran to Gokalpuri and Dayalpur police stations crying out for help. But each time they found the gates locked from the inside. For three days, no help came.... Since the riots broke out in Delhi at the end of February, the worst religious conflict to engulf the capital in decades, questions have persisted about the role that the Delhi police played in enabling the violence, which was predominately Hindu mobs attacking Muslims. Of the 51 people who died, at least three-quarters were Muslim, and many Muslims are still missing.

[↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-NYTimes-Analysis-March1_79-0)Gettleman, Jeffrey; Abi-Habib, Maria (1 March 2020), ["In India, Modi's Policies Have Lit a Fuse"](https://www.nytimes.com/2020/03/01/world/asia/india-modi-hindus.html), _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_, [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200301173003/https://www.nytimes.com/2020/03/01/world/asia/india-modi-hindus.html) from the original on 1 March 2020, retrieved 1 March 2020, This past week, as neighborhoods in India's capital burned and religiously driven bloodletting consumed more than 40 lives, most of them Muslim, India's government was quick to say that the violence was spontaneous... Many Muslims are now leaving, hoisting their unburned things on their heads and trudging away from streets that still smell of smoke.
078. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-nytimes-2020-3-12-two-thirds_80-0)Gettleman, Jeffrey; Yasir, Sameer; Raj, Suhasini; Kumar, Hari (12 March 2020), ["'If We Kill You, Nothing Will Happen': How Delhi's Police Turned Against Muslims"](https://www.nytimes.com/2020/03/12/world/asia/india-police-muslims.html), _The New York Times_, Photographs by Loke, Atul, [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200313011029/https://www.nytimes.com/2020/03/12/world/asia/india-police-muslims.html) from the original on 13 March 2020, retrieved 13 March 2020, Two-thirds of the more than 50 people who were killed and have been identified were Muslim.
079. [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-wapo-3-6-20-slater-1_81-0) [2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi#cite_ref-wapo-3-6-20-slater-1_81-1)Slater, Joanna; Masih, Niha (6 March 2020), ["In Delhi's worst violence in decades, a man watched his brother burn"](https://www.washingtonpost.com/world/asia_pacific/in-delhis-worst-violence-in-decades-a-man-watched-his-brother-burn/2020/03/05/892dbb12-5e45-11ea-ac50-18701e14e06d_story.html), _The Washington Post_, [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200307070624/https://www.washingtonpost.com/world/asia_pacific/in-delhis-worst-violence-in-decades-a-man-watched-his-brother-burn/2020/03/05/892dbb12-5e45-11ea-ac50-18701e14e06d_story.html) from the original on 7 March 2020, retrieved 6 March 2020, At least 53 people were killed or suffered deadly injuries in violence that persisted for two days. The majority of those killed were Muslims, many shot, hacked or burned to death. A police officer and an intelligence officer were also killed. So too were more than a dozen Hindus, most of them shot or assaulted.

She said mobs entered her lane shouting 'Jai Shri Ram,' or 'Victory to Lord Ram,' a slogan favoured by Modi's party, and demanded to know which houses were occupied by Muslims. She said she saw a neighbour set on fire in front of her, an account repeated by other witnesses.
